# Open findings register

Things measured, recorded, and **not fixed** — with no line item in any stage
of [NSOT_PLAN.md](NSOT_PLAN.md).

**Why this file exists.** Each of these was written into `CLAUDE.md` or a
stage document at the moment it was found, in prose, beside the thing it was
found next to. That is the right place to explain *why* something is true and
the wrong place to keep a list: prose accumulates invisibly, and the only way
to know what is outstanding was to have been present when each one was
recorded. The register is the list; the prose stays where it is and is linked.

**Rules.** An item leaves this file by being fixed, by being scheduled into a
stage, or by being closed with a reason. **A row that is fixed moves to
Closed in the same turn** (2026-09-28: 31 of 92 rows in the open sections
were done and had never moved, so the open count read about twice the work;
`tests/test_register_hygiene.py` now refuses a done row in an open
section). **A live exposure is listed here even when a stage owns it**: a
row with a home stopped counting as a finding, and NSOT_PLAN 6.1 sat outside
the triage built to catch it (C143). It does not leave by being
forgotten.

**The open count is a claim about DEFERRED work** (the operator, 2026-09-28). A row a stage owns lives in *Scheduled out of the register*, naming the stage, and leaves the open count; the stage's plan lists it. The open sections hold only what waits: A, B, UNKNOWN, and C deferred to Stage 9. Twice the register misled us about its own size (31 done rows never moved; then 26 of 61 open rows that were stage work), and both times the fix was a rule a test enforces: `test_register_hygiene.py` refuses an open row whose bucket places it in a stage other than Stage 9.

**The stopping rule for a sweep** (the operator, 2026-09-28, applied by default). A sweep may REGISTER whatever it finds, and only A items it finds are fixed in the same pass. Everything else waits, however cheap and however near the code being edited. A items found by fixing A items had begun to feed themselves (C155, C157, C158, C160), and "we are already in the file" is how a fix grows a second fix nobody triaged. C161 is the rule's first application: found inside C160's fix, in the same function, and left. Anything recorded as *"not applied"*, *"noted, not yet
addressed"*, *"recorded as a gap"* or *"left open"* belongs here on the same
day it is written.

**A register is only current if the reader checks the head.** A stale read
of a maintained file cannot be told apart from a stale file. On 2026-09-25 this
register was read at `1b9b4d3` while the repository was four commits ahead,
and three items already closed were raised again as open. That is the failure
this file exists to prevent, arriving through the reader rather than the
document. Before acting on an item, run `git log -1 -- docs/OPEN_FINDINGS.md`
and compare it with the head you are reading.

Status: **open** unless stated. Last reviewed 2026-09-28 (the triage below).

## Triage, 2026-09-28: every open row placed

**The rule (the operator's): a finding's SEVERITY is a claim about the world,
and claims about the world are measured, not assumed.** The findings that
turned out to be live exposure all read as tidying until something was
measured: C95 (masking, and it was a secret in a shared system), C141 (a code
default, and it was a published value) and C136 (cosmetic, and the import
could not be performed for 9 hours). The inverse held too (C135, C138, C142
real and harmless). So the classification depends on facts, and a row whose
severity rests on an unmeasured one is UNKNOWN until it is measured.

**The buckets, each a stated criterion:**
- **A, LIVE EXPOSURE**: true in the world now, and time makes it worse: a
  secret readable, a device unmanageable, data being lost.
- **B, BLOCKING**: something specific cannot be built or run until it is
  fixed. The row names what. If nothing is blocked, it is not B.
- **UNKNOWN**: could be A, and the fact that decides it has not been
  measured. Named with the one measurement and its cost.
- **C, EVERYTHING ELSE**: real, correctly recorded, and the tool works
  without it.

**Before sorting:** 92 rows sat in the open sections. **31 were already done**
and moved to Closed: 29 on their own row's evidence (FIXED, DONE or closed in
the status cell, never moved), and 2 measured done on the host tonight (C17's
URLs are set; E4's drift checker is enabled and ran at 05:47Z). C141 was
merged into C39, and C143 was added. **61 open.**

### Re-sort, 2026-09-28: the CURRENT buckets, and why the triage had stopped

**The operator's question: was the triage a one-time sort, or do new rows
default to C? It was a one-time sort.** The re-triage's split (functional,
lab, minor) answers WHERE work goes, and it silently replaced the severity
axis (A, B, UNKNOWN, C) for every row recorded after it: C144, C145, C147 and
C148 each got a home and no bucket. By the criterion, **C148 is B (it blocks
the onboard-to-deploy loop, the product's central one)**, and **C147 was A
when it was recorded** (latent: the next device arriving with an RW community
would have had it written into the first permanent record; fixed the same
turn).

**Now structural:** every row in a live section, "Scheduled" included, opens
its status cell with ONE bucket, `**[A|B|UNKNOWN|C …; sorted <date>]**`. B
names what it blocks and whether that is on the critical path; UNKNOWN names
the measurement that would settle it; C names its kind (F functional, L
deferred for the lab, M minor) and its home.
`tests/test_register_hygiene.py` refuses a row without one, with a control.
**A new finding gets its bucket the turn it is recorded, with the criterion
applied, never assumed.**

**The current counts (55 open):**

| Bucket | Rows | Count |
|---|---|---|
| **A** | C39, C139, C143, all deferred (L) by the operator's decision, and COUPLED (6.1 before the rotation) | **3** |
| **B, critical** | C148 (the onboard-to-deploy loop), C54 (7.2), C50 (7.3) | **3** |
| **B, later** | C10 (7.4), C7 (7.7), C60 (Stage 8), C100 (7.10's adoption), E6 (8.7) | **5** |
| **UNKNOWN** | C78, C93, each with its measurement in its row (C106 was here; corrected below) | **2** |
| **C** | 41 open, plus the six in "Scheduled" | **41** |

**Corrected the same night: C106 was never UNKNOWN, and the error was in the
instrument.** Its two "unknowns" were (2), fixed on 09-27 (`bfde4ef`,
`d7efb9d`), and (3), already read from the code and written in the row. The
triage sorted every row from a dump that CUT EACH CELL AT 420 CHARACTERS, and
C106's finding is 7,228: the answers sat past the cut. So every live row was
re-scanned in full for a bucket-changing word past the cut. C106 was the only
row hiding a FIXED, and C93's hidden measurement leaves its UNKNOWN standing
(the prober is ruled out, and the lab host's load has no history). **A bucket
assigned from a truncated read is a claim about the truncation.**

**The inverse check (anything in C that should be A or B), row by row:**
- **D8 was CLOSED in its own row and sat in "Scheduled"**, a section the
  hygiene test did not cover. Moved. The test's first extension still missed
  it (it read the third cell, and "Scheduled" keeps its status in the fourth),
  and the control against the old register is what showed that.
- **C96 became visible on every run** once E4 put the drift checker back on
  (a run whose drifted devices are drawn nowhere). Still C: it is 7.2's
  consumer, and a detection shown incompletely is not a loss. It is flagged
  in its bucket.
- The "later" B rows (C60, C100, E6) had been folded into C as "effectively
  C". The criterion makes them B; their not being on the critical path is
  said in the bucket, not by dropping them from it.
- Nothing else in C met A: no store losing data (job health 24 of 24), no
  device unmanageable (startup-check 9 of 9), and no secret readable beyond
  the three A rows.

## B. Secrets and audit surfaces

| # | Finding | Kind | Where recorded |
|---|---|---|---|
| B9 | **CONFIRMED 2026-09-26: the off-box B2 key can destroy the backups it writes, without `deleteFiles`.** Run by the operator: `rclone delete -vv --include b2probe.txt` printed `Deleted` with no refusal, the normal listing was empty, and `--b2-versions` held the hidden version. `writeFiles` allows a HIDE, and the lifecycle deletes hidden versions a day later, so a compromised NMAS erases every off-box copy within 24 h. The first test (a single-file path: exit 0, file still listed) passed while proving nothing, because rclone's single-file check needs `readFiles`, so the file was never touched. **Fix: B2 Object Lock, Governance mode, default retention 16 days: the lifecycle's hide-after (15) plus its hide-to-delete (1)**, so no hide can delete anything sooner than the lifecycle would have. (First recorded as 15, which was a day short; corrected 2026-09-26) (docs/NETBOX_BACKUP.md §4). The semantics are from B2's documentation as understood here, and a five-step probe plan measures them before real data depends on them. **Closes when** step 4 of that plan shows a hidden LOCKED version surviving the lifecycle, and step 5 shows a governance bypass works. | **[C, L (Object Lock enabled, by the operator's account; not re-measured here); sorted 2026-09-28]** operator's step (measure, then enable) | docs/NETBOX_BACKUP.md §4 |
| B10 | **Nothing checks that the bucket's lock retention covers its lifecycle window.** **Decided 2026-09-26 (operator): a read-only key**, scoped to the bucket, which is ALSO the retrieval key (docs/NETBOX_BACKUP.md §6): one key, on the laptop to fetch and on the NMAS to check. It may live on the NMAS because everything it can read, the NMAS already holds unencrypted. The key exists (2026-09-26), and its capabilities include `readBucketLifecycleRules`, so the lifecycle half is readable. Whether it can read the Object Lock retention is unknown until the check is built against the real response. It also passed retrieval end to end (docs/NETBOX_BACKUP.md 6f), and its attempt to hide was refused with a 401. It cannot write, so it cannot undermine what it checks, and a compromised NMAS can use it only to look. A manual check in the B2 UI was rejected: it is done once and never again, and this pair is exactly what drifts when one is changed and the other forgotten. **The property to check is not "both are 15"** but **lock retention >= daysFromUploadingToHiding + daysFromHidingToDeleting**. Shorter fails, because files become deletable before the lifecycle removes them: the hole re-opening silently. Longer warns, because the lock has silently become the retention. Equal passes. **Build after** the lock is enabled and the key exists, against the real `b2_list_buckets` response: building against documented field shapes is how B6's first read reported `never` for two VMs that had images. The new key goes into `nmas-check-secret-storage`'s declared list. | **[C, M; sorted 2026-09-28]** build | docs/NETBOX_BACKUP.md §4 |
| C143 | **oxidized-web serves every device's running configuration, unauthenticated, to the whole LAN: NSOT_PLAN's 6.1, parked "on its own schedule" since 2026-09-25 and never measured again** (found by the triage's own question, which open item is true in the world now, 2026-09-28; the register searched first: no row). Measured from the laptop, an ordinary LAN host (<laptop>), by TCP connect: ports 8888 (oxidized-web), 8000 (NetBox), 3100 (Loki) and 9116 (the SNMP exporter) on the NMAS host are OPEN; only NMAS's own 5000 is filtered. `GET /nodes.json` on 8888 answered 200 with no credential asked (status and size read; no config fetched), so every device's config, with its `secret 9` hashes and the live community, is one request away for anyone on the LAN. **Two consequences:** C95's masking in NetBox protects nothing against a LAN reader while Oxidized serves the same values unmasked; and **a community rotation made now would publish the NEW value on the LAN the day it lands**, so 6.1 precedes the rotation. NetBox's API refuses without a token (403). Loki answers unauthenticated: its reads expose device logs, and whether its PUSH is open (a LAN host writing heartbeat lines that make a dead device look alive) is not measured, and is not to be measured by writing. The exporter answers anyone, so it will SNMP-query a target with the fleet's community on a stranger's behalf. **Why it had no row:** the register holds what has no stage, and 6.1 had one. A live exposure sat in a plan under "its own schedule", which is no schedule: a stage number is not a date. | **[A, deferred (L): every device's config served to the LAN, unauthenticated; sorted 2026-09-28]** **DEFERRED, lab (the operator, 2026-09-28): Stage 9's "safe here because it is a lab" half.** Real exposure, acceptable in this environment only: a lab, no hostile party on the LAN, emulated and disposable devices. It moves ahead of whatever stage is running if the tool manages anything real, if the lab becomes reachable beyond the operator's LAN, or if a device holds a credential that also works somewhere else (NSOT_PLAN Stage 9). **6.1 (C143) and the community rotation (C139) are COUPLED; whichever happens first constrains the other.** Rotating while oxidized-web still serves configs to the LAN publishes the NEW value there the day it lands. Closing 6.1 changes what reads Oxidized and NetBox (NMAS reads NetBox at the LAN address), which the rotation's consumer work must also match. Was TRIAGE A. The step, when it is taken: measure every consumer's URL first (NMAS's `oxidized_url`, the clab host's reads, Grafana's and Prometheus's sources), then bind each published port to 127.0.0.1 or add `DOCKER-USER` rules, then re-measure FROM ANOTHER HOST (6.1's own acceptance). Before the rotation. | [NSOT_PLAN.md](NSOT_PLAN.md) 6.1 |
| C144 | **The daily restore test was never evidence that the ENCRYPTED copies can be read, and its green light was read that way** (the operator, 2026-09-28, closing B8). `nmas-netbox-restore-test` restores the newest LOCAL hourly backup, which is plain, and the VM holds only the public key, so it decrypts nothing: a pass proves the dump restores, and nothing about Proxmox or B2. The operator's reading ("something decrypts these") is the claim the test's NAME makes and its code does not: a restore test beside encrypted off-box copies reads as covering them. The one assertion about them is the empty-keyring decrypt (NETBOX_BACKUP 6g), proven 2026-09-28. It needs the passphrase, so no job can run it unattended. | **[C, M → Stage 9; sorted 2026-09-28, re-sorted the same day (the operator): nothing blocks on it, and "placed in 7.2" was a stage it was not in]** The HONESTY half is DONE: the restore test's own job-health row now says it covers the plain local copy only. Remaining, Stage 9: a job-health row stating when an off-box copy was last PROVEN readable by 6g and by whom, entered by the person who ran it, stale after the interval the operator sets (proposed: 90 days); the restore test's own row states that it covers the plain local copy only | [NETBOX_BACKUP.md](NETBOX_BACKUP.md) 6g, "What this does not give" |
| C145 | **Importing the backup key's OpenPGP export into an empty keyring fails, unexplained, so the `paperkey` file is UNPROVEN as a copy** (the operator's run, 2026-09-28). `gpg --export-secret-keys` gave real, iter+salt S2K-protected material for both keys (parsed: never `gnu-dummy` or divert-to-card), and `gpg --import` into an empty `GNUPGHOME` refused the cv25519 subkey (`error sending to agent: Bad secret key`), after which the decrypt said `No SmartCard daemon`, with no card configured and no stub on the laptop. Not told apart: a mistyped passphrase at the import prompt, or GnuPG's conversion of this subkey. B8 was closed by routing around it (the agent's own key files need no import). `paperkey` restores through the same import, so NETBOX_BACKUP's "restore-tested paperkey file" is a claim this measurement does not support until the paperkey passes 6g's empty-keyring decrypt. | **[C, M; sorted 2026-09-28]** (M) minor, Stage 9 candidate: run 6g from the paperkey; if it fails, diagnose the import (the passphrase first, then GnuPG's version and the subkey) | [NETBOX_BACKUP.md](NETBOX_BACKUP.md) custody, 6g |
| C156 | **probe-r1a's credential override disappeared from the store with no code that removes it and no trace of what did** (found in R1's teardown, 2026-09-28; the register searched first). Measured on the host: at the teardown-prep read the override keys were `10.255.0.32` and `10.255.0.33`; the operator's clear printed `before: False`; the backup it took first (`cp -p`, so the SOURCE's mtime) shows the store last written at **17:15:28**, already without `.33`; the list deletion ran at 17:16:06 (so the operator's reading, "cleared by the list deletion", does not fit the order) and does not touch the store in code. Only `clear_device_override()` pops an override, and only retire and Abandon call it, neither run then. At 17:15:28 the app log holds only a `GET /status/10.255.0.33` poll, 16 s after the second deploy restart, and the journal nothing. **Clears are not logged** (a set is), so the store cannot say who removed what. Harmless here (the device was being torn down); the concern is that a credential can leave the store without a cause anyone can find. | **[UNKNOWN: which process wrote the store at 17:15:28, and what it dropped. The measurement: every credential-store write logged with the process, the caller and the keys it adds and removes (never values), then watch; C157's cross-process lost update is the leading candidate; sorted 2026-09-28]** | [credentials.py](../modules/credentials.py) `_load`, `_save` |

| C93 | **s3, the manager's gateway, forwards for the whole fleet while failing to answer for itself** (found inside C92's measurement; the operator's emphasis, 2026-09-27). Every other device's traffic to the NMAS crosses s3's `Vlan99`, and in 106 hours of the host's ping log s3 missed a probe 1,540 times, about one every four minutes; 1,362 of its 1,478 single-probe misses came with no other device down. So its data plane worked while its control plane did not answer ICMP or TCP:22 within 2 s. It is its own row, not a line in C92, because it is not about the dot: s3 is the one device where a surprise costs the whole lab, and a measured responsiveness problem on it had been seen by nothing. **The clock correlation** (s3's clock runs at 0.55 to 0.61, s4's at 0.75, and they miss most: 1,540 and 227 against 161 to 192 for every other device) is suggestive, not a cause. **It predicts something testable**: if a starved emulator is the cause, s3's misses track the clab VM's load (which sat at 15 or more overnight). **Measured the same day, the competing explanation is ruled out**: the PROBER is not starved. The NMAS host's load at s3's misses (median 0.56, p90 1.04) matches its load over the whole span (0.55, 1.06), and s3's misses per hour against the host's hourly load give a Spearman of 0.07 over 108 hours. **The prediction itself cannot be tested from stored data**: Prometheus's node exporter covers the NMAS host only (`localhost:9100`), so the clab VM's load has no history anywhere this tool can read. **Also open, not filed: the 92-minute s3 outage at 2026-09-27 02:34**, during the ZTP work. The transition log cannot tell "s3 was down" from "the app stopped", which is itself the finding: a reader that logs only transitions cannot distinguish its own absence from its subject's. **The misses are stalls, not slowness** (the operator's arithmetic, 2026-09-27). r2 answers in 23 to 31 ms across s3's Vlan99, and the probe gives up only after 2 s: about 65 to 85 times the normal round trip. On the host the probe is TCP:22 alone (C92: `ping3` cannot open a socket there). **Corrected the same day (C97):** a miss is EITHER 2 s with no answer OR an instant refusal, and the second is what r2 did when every vty line was held by the tool's own leaked sessions. A refused connect returns in milliseconds, so it is not a stall at all. The probe records neither kind, so "s3 has multi-second stalls" is one of two readings of its 1,540 misses, not a finding. s3 has sixteen vty lines, which makes exhaustion harder than on a five-line router, not impossible. Either way it matters beyond the dot, because every SSH session the tool opens to s3 meets whichever it is. | **[UNKNOWN: are s3's missed probes load on the lab host? (C94's metrics, then a day); sorted 2026-09-28]** measure, then decide: put a node exporter (or `sar`) on the clab VM, then correlate s3's misses with its load; the C92 reader records its own liveness, which closes the outage gap for the future | this file, 2026-09-27<br>**C188's measurement adds a data point (2026-09-29, the operator)**: s3 was the slowest device every time and got SLOWER under concurrency, 23 to 24 s read alone and 40.9 s read beside the other eight. That fits this row's hypothesis: every other device's session crosses s3's `Vlan99`, and vIOS forwards it in software on the CPU answering s3's own session, so reading nine at once makes s3 forward eight sessions while serving a ninth. Its clock (0.55 to 0.61) is the fleet's slowest. **Whether s3 needs more resources stays open, and stays in Stage 9**: if s1 to s4 share one allocation and only s3 is slow, the cause is its WORKLOAD (the gateway role), not its allocation, and more vCPU on a host at load 15 buys a slice of a busy machine for a largely single-threaded emulator. `docker stats` during a Save All settles it, which is C94's scope. Redeploying s3 is the riskiest change in the lab: it carries every other device's management path. **Measured 2026-09-29 (the operator's Save All): s3 is the fleet's slowest to CONNECT**, 13.7 s at the preview, and the apply's connect failed after 11.2 s. That failure is the tool's 10 s connect bound firing below s3's connect (C205), not evidence that s3 was down; the connection itself, not the show, is now where s3 is worst. |
| C94 | **The lab host is unmonitored: the machine running every device in the lab has no metrics collected at all** (the operator, 2026-09-27, from C93's measurement). It changes what retire does to a SHARED system, so retire's preview must say so.  **Found planning the rotation, 2026-09-28, before anything moved:** (a) **through the deploy path the rotation is ADD-ONLY** (C12's class, its second member): merge-only never negates, so new intent adds the new `snmp-server community` line and leaves the old value (`public`) enabled beside it. The rotation finishes only with a removal: the device's own old line negated VERBATIM, as onboarding already removes the RW community (a rebuilt line drops the ACL and does not match). That removal needs a path the deploy does not have. (b) **Every consumer moves in the same window, or monitoring goes dark**: in the repo, `collector_config.get_snmp_community()` and `snmp_collector.get_device_summary(..., community="public")`, whose hard-coded default is the old value; outside it, the Prometheus SNMP exporter's config on the host (not yet measured). Decide the removal path and list the consumers before rotating.  **DECIDED 2026-09-28 (the operator):** removal by extending onboarding's verbatim removal into a rotation operation (it removes the exact line it read, never a synthesised `no`, the discipline of `rollback_commands()`, leaving merge-only intact; no Mode B), which reads, removes, then READS BACK (the persist/C112 rule); add and remove ONE operation per device; consumers measured first, all changed to read the new value from ONE place, then the devices; s4 first. **Consumers MEASURED on the host 2026-09-28 (read-only, by hash):** (1) the SNMP exporter (docker `prom/snmp-exporter:v0.29.0`, :9116) reads `/etc/snmp_exporter/snmp.yml`, whose auths `public_v1`/`public_v2` hold the fleet value; four Prometheus jobs use `public_v2` (cisco_8000v 5 targets, cisco_vios_l2 4, cisco_ipsla 5, lldp 9); the auth's NAME states the value. (2) `snmptrapd` (udp 162, `authCommunity`) holds it, and all eight devices send traps there (`snmp-server host 10.255.1.10`): after a rotation their traps are dropped with nothing saying so. (3) **NMAS's own SNMP reads no configured value at all**: no list has a `collector_config.json`, so `get_snmp_community()` returns its literal fallback, and `snmp_get`/`snmp_walk`/`get_device_summary` default to the same literal; it works only because the fleet's value EQUALS the code's default (measured: equal). A secret whose owner is a default argument: one owner per fact, like `tftp_server_ip` (C48). (4) The value is held per device in the credential store as `default:<host>:snmp_community_ro`, nine copies, r5's included (a retired device's secret still stored). (5) `/etc/prometheus/snmp.yml` holds it and NOTHING reads it (the apt exporter unit is inactive; the container mounts `/etc/snmp_exporter`): a copy of a secret in a dead file. (6) **Not consumers, measured:** Telegraf (active inputs are host metrics and MDT on :57000, no SNMP), Oxidized (SSH), and the host's own `snmpd` (its own agent; its config is unreadable to this account, so "not the fleet's value" is UNPROVEN, not shown).  **The survey completed 2026-09-28 (the operator: the list must be complete):** (7) the host's own `snmpd` ANSWERS the fleet's value (v1 and v2c) on localhost only and refuses a random one (the control), measured by asking it since its config is unreadable to this account; none of Prometheus's four SNMP jobs targets it, so it is not a consumer of the devices' community: the same value, as an install default, on its own agent. Its own decision, named so the list is complete. (8) The literal: introduced once (`beec347`, 2026-04-10) and never anything but the fleet's value; see C141 for the repository. **DECIDED (the operator): the file_sd / per-device-secret design.** The transitional state is what a per-device rotation IS, so every consumer holds the value per device: NMAS reads each device's own secret (the credential store intent renders from) and the literal defaults go; Prometheus's SNMP targets move to a `file_sd` file NMAS writes, each target labelled with its auth; the exporter's `snmp.yml` and `snmptrapd.conf` hold one entry per value in use, written by a host script run with sudo that writes, reloads, READS BACK and says what the service now holds. Between s4 and the other eight: prove BOTH halves, s4 polled and trapped on the new value AND the other eight still polled after the exporter's move to `file_sd`.  **Step 1 BUILT 2026-09-28: NMAS reads each device's own secret.** `collector_config.device_community()` / `community_for_address()`, no fallback (a device with no secret is refused by name); the literal defaults are gone from `snmp_get`, `snmp_walk` and `get_device_summary` (an AST check forbids a default on any `community` parameter); the poll route and the agent's two SNMP tools take no community from the request or the model; the monitoring form's RO/RW fields, a second owner nothing had set, are gone and a request carrying one is refused naming the owner. **Found building it:** the agent's `snmp_poll` printed `community=<value>` into what the provider receives, a prose form positional redaction cannot see, on a success branch the leak sweep cannot reach because no test device answers SNMP (C56's class); it no longer prints it, pinned through the real `run_chat()` loop. | C148 | **NO ONBOARDED DEVICE CAN BE DEPLOYED TO FROM THE INTERFACE: the path from "onboarded" to "the tool can deploy to it" does not exist in the product** (found by R1, trying to use it; recorded as its own row at the operator's direction, 2026-09-28). Phase 1 commits a bootstrap-shaped intent (probe-r1a: `bootstrap`, `hostname`, `logging`, `secret_refs`; none of `interfaces`, `routing`, `users`), a deploy needs full intent, and seeding full intent from the golden (extract, review, commit) has no GUI (`templatize.commit_extraction` is in `NO_GUI`). The plan's "curl-only today" was wrong: the commit route needs a VERIFIED PERSON, so a curl from the host is refused. So every onboarding produces a device the tool cannot yet manage. **Measured on the host, how each device's intent first arrived** (first commit of each `host_vars/` file): r1–r4 and s1–s4 by `host_vars: … commit reviewed extraction` on 2026-09-20/21, `Source: extraction`, no `Actor-Verified` (before P.3); r6 by `onboarding: r6` (bootstrap, 0 of 3 full-intent keys) at 2026-09-24 05:32, then `commit reviewed extraction` at 21:04 with `Actor: user` TYPED INTO THE REQUEST BODY, verified by nothing, which made it full. **So the tool took a device from onboarding to deployable exactly once (r6, 2026-09-24), through the extraction route while it was ungated; P.3 (2026-09-26) gated that route correctly and left no path in its place.** The fleet is deployable because its intent was extracted before the gate, not because any supported path produces it now. **Stated plainly (the operator): the product has NEVER had a working path from onboarding to deployable. It had an UNGATED one, used once (r6, `Actor: user` verified by nothing). "The tool can onboard and deploy" has been true of the FLEET and never true of the PRODUCT.** The third run in a week to find the break AROUND the operation rather than in it: C70 (restore's surroundings), R2a (the import's), R1 (onboarding's output). | **[B, CRITICAL: blocks the onboard-to-deploy loop, the product's central one (7.3 first); sorted 2026-09-28; BUILT 2026-09-28 as 7.3 step 1, its acceptance run the operator's]** **Built** (`modules/nsot/seed.py`, `POST /templatize/seed/preview` and `/apply`, the Device page's Seed intent): each device's COMMITTED golden parsed into the intent document, previewed against what is committed now, with what the template does not model named line by line (an unmodelled line round-trips verbatim at 100% and still blocks a deploy until acknowledged, measured on r2 with one planted: `ok`, 100.0%, 99.3% modelled); confirmed by a hash of the document AND the golden (the document holds refs, never values); parsed again at apply; ONE commit of exactly the seeded files as the verified person, `Source: seed`, one `Seeded-From: <device>@<golden>` trailer each. Only a device whose intent is absent or only the bootstrap is seeded: over full intent it would replace what the device SHOULD be with what it IS, so that device is shown, not selectable, and the apply checks again (the hash cannot see intent committed in between; a control removing that check was caught only by a test added for it). A template that does not model the device does not block the seed: the deploy's own gate does, per device, and the seed says so. The four routes it replaces (extract, staged, rendered, the reviewed commit) are removed, and with them a defect found reading them: extraction resolved the device from the ACTIVE list's inventory, not the list it was given; the seed carries its list from the preview. The deploy plan's bootstrap refusal now names the Device page's Seed intent. Also found: C175. **What remains is the acceptance**, C117's loop on a fresh throwaway, onboard -> seed -> deploy -> break -> roll back. The original plan: build (functional, CRITICAL PATH, 7.3 FIRST): seed intent from the golden as an operation (preview, confirm by hash, result, record), the step that makes onboarding useful rather than one of five Device-page actions. Acceptance: C117's loop on a fresh throwaway, onboard → seed → deploy → break → roll back | [NSOT_STAGE7_PLAN.md](NSOT_STAGE7_PLAN.md) 7.3 |
| C172 | **A list's `remote.json` that cannot be read is treated as NO remote, so its pushes stop in silence, and it is written through a shared `.tmp`** (read 2026-09-28 building C171's probe; the register searched first). `remote.load_remote()` catches any error, logs it and returns `None`, the same answer as an absent file, and a list with no remote is quiet by design, so a damaged record reads as a list nobody gave a remote. `save_remote()` writes `<path>.tmp` then renames: C20's and C158's shared-temp shape, and `record_push` / `record_push_failure` read-modify-write it with no lock. `remote_path()` resolves through `get_list_data_dir()`, which creates the directory (the C51 family) on a read. C158's 85-site write survey is where it should have been caught. | **[C, M; sorted 2026-09-28]** the tool works today (every record reads); the fix is filestore's read-for-write and a distinct unreadable state. **Its SYMPTOM is visible since C223 (2026-09-29)**: the `remote-publication` reader compares HEAD with the remote through the repository's own `origin`, so pushes that stop because the record cannot be read show as commits not pushed, and the row names C172; the record itself is not fixed | `modules/nsot/remote.py`; `modules/filestore.py` |
| C174 | **NetBox scoping: tenants against regions, and per-list containers** (the operator, 2026-09-28: deferred, recorded with both sides). **The case for tenants (the operator):** a tenant means "who this belongs to", which is what a list is; regions mean geography and should stay for geography; every object carries a tenant field natively; and two lists could each have a site called "HQ", which region-named sites cannot hold. NetBox filters by tenant throughout its UI, so "this network's objects" is one click. Regions-as-lists spends the geographic hierarchy on something that is not geography, so a list whose devices are genuinely in two places cannot say so. And the measurement below makes the move cheap either way, which the operator named as the better reason to defer than any argument about which model is right. **The migration's questions, measured:** the sync writes one region and one site per list, both named after the list (`_ensure_region`, `_ensure_site`); the provenance record keys on object ids per type, `dcim/regions` included, and removal's order deletes regions; nothing else reads the region (only `netbox_client.py` and `netbox_guard.py` name it). Per-list NetBox CONTAINERS (five kinds per list) are the same treatment. | **[C, M → Stage 9; sorted 2026-09-28]** decide later. Placed in Stage 9, not the end of Stage 7: P.8 adds the per-list `netbox_scope` key that every writer reads, so after P.8 a tenant move changes a value and the scope reader, not every writer. P.8 makes it cheaper, so it waits without its cost growing. Regions work and the model does its job | `modules/netbox_client.py`; NSOT_PLAN P.8 |

| E5 | **Authorization: roles and separation of duties are DECIDED and unbuilt.** Any verified person may do every gate kind today. The decision covers the roles, the split of `approve` into `author` and `approve`, per-artifact separation of duties, host-side storage, and the `any_person` default. Two parts are needed before any build: measure whether Access tokens carry groups, and D10's trailer. Stage 7 already draws for it (pattern 6). **Attribution now serves two purposes** (the operator, 2026-09-27): D10's `Actor-Verified` and P.3's person-gated confirms were built as security controls, and they are also the foundation of 8.8's written override reasons, an ACCOUNTABILITY control counted per person and per device. An override attributed to "user" or "pipeline" would be worthless. The second purpose is why attribution stays strict where the security argument alone might have relaxed it, and any change to the role model is checked against both. | **[C, L; sorted 2026-09-28]** build | [NSOT_AUTHORIZATION.md](NSOT_AUTHORIZATION.md); NSOT_PLAN.md *AUTHZ* |
| E6 | **Make the DEVICE the witness of its own config changes: `archive` / `log config` / `notify syslog`** (pulled forward by the operator, 2026-09-27, from 8.7's checklist). Measured: RESTCONF on r1-r4 and r6, NETCONF on those five and s1, and no device logs its own config changes, so there are writable paths to the fleet that the pipeline does not own and nothing witnesses. A device-side change log is the only control that closes that gap however the change arrives, and it feeds 8.6 (who changed it) and 8.7 (positive attribution). It is a syslog change, so it goes through INTENT the way P.1's block did. The pipeline exists, and so does P.1b's bulk intent. **Not modelled today**: no parser, template or fixture carries `archive`, so the parser, template and round-trip change comes first (the decision rule: every device, so it is modelled). Then measure arrival per device, with a floor, as the heartbeat was. | **[B, later: blocks 8.7; (L); sorted 2026-09-28]** build | [NSOT_PLAN.md](NSOT_PLAN.md) 8.7 |

None of them blocks Stage 7 — the per-stage scope and the ordering
constraints are in [NSOT_PLAN.md](NSOT_PLAN.md), *Scope of what remains*,
where **Stage 5's count is flagged as a reading of an acceptance paragraph
rather than an enumeration**: it is the one figure there with a different
provenance from the rest, and enumerating Stage 5 is the first item of Stage 5.

**A1 was the one whose absence makes other work untrustworthy** rather than merely incomplete: a mistake in NetBox has nothing
to restore from. It is now P.2, scheduled ahead of Stage 7 for that reason.

C1 was the second such item until it was measured, and the correction is worth
keeping: a finding recorded as a defect became the explanation for the next
confusing output, and that output was correct behaviour. *A pattern that has
been right four times is exactly the one to distrust on the fifth* — the
register makes a finding easier to find, and easier to reach for.

| C178 | **Verify passes a BGP break that has not yet reset the session: it returns at its FIRST passing read, 10 s in, while IOS holds a session to its 180 s hold time** (found reading `convergence.wait_for` for C117's unknown (1), 2026-09-28). `wait_for` returns `converged` at the first read satisfying its check, and BGP's first read comes at `initial_wait` (10 s); the window's 60 s is the most it WAITS for a recovery, never how long it WATCHES. A deploy that breaks BGP without resetting the TCP session at once (a filter on TCP 179, a lost route to a multihop peer, perhaps the update-source going down: C117's (1)) reads Established at 10 s, passes, rolls back nothing, and the receipt records the checks as passed. It is the verify's claim outrunning what it looked at: "verified" means "up 10 s after the push". The tenth verify defect (C62, C64 to C68, C108, C114, C115), and the operator will measure it on the throwaway before `timers` is added, so it is confirmed on a real device rather than inferred from the code. The hold time is the CONFIGURED one (`neighbor timers`, then peer-group, then `timers bgp`, then IOS's 180 s), an upper bound on the negotiated one, so the wait is conservative. **Cost, stated**: a deploy or restore to a BGP device (r3, r4) now takes up to 180 s longer. (1) **Done 2026-09-29**: r3's `show bgp all neighbors` shows hold time 180 and keepalive 60 on both sessions, so the 180 s wait is the right bound there. And one fact on the live fleet: the IPv6 peer reads "Route to peer address reachability Up: 1; Down: 1" with "Connections established 1; dropped 0", so the route to the peer went down once and the SESSION NEVER DROPPED. That is IOS holding a session through a lost route, supporting C178's premise, not proof, since how long the route was down is not known. Awaiting (2) C117's real-device loop (does a filter on TCP 179 hold the session to hold expiry). The other folder (`Interface down`, `Critical syslog received`, `Device unreachable (SNMP)`, the telemetry and IP SLA rules) was built by hand in earlier lab work, and nothing has ever checked any of it; of the two examined, one could never fire (C166) and one could not be tested (C167). `Device unreachable (SNMP)` shows 9 normal instances and `Interface error rate elevated` 73, which proves they EVALUATE and says nothing about whether they can FIRE: C166 evaluated happily against a condition it could never meet. **It bears on 8.6**: the triage reader consumes these rules, and a reader is only as good as the rules it reads. The durable direction, the heartbeat rules' precedent: rules as code, generated and tested against real captured lines, so a rule that cannot match what the fleet produces fails a test instead of reading as quiet. **SURVEYED 2026-09-28 (probes 5 and 6, run on the host, read-only through Grafana's proxy; 7 days of each rule's own query, 30 days of state history).** Seven rules:<br>- `Device unreachable (SNMP)`, `up < 1` for 2 m: CAN fire (`up` reached 0 in 7 days); 54 Normal and 46 Pending, never Alerting, so every dip was shorter than 2 minutes;<br>- `Interface down`, `ifOperStatus > 1 and ifAdminStatus == 1`: its inputs exist (73 series each), the condition never held in 7 days, and it has alerted 6 times in 30. **Not blind: quiet**, and its no-data setting is `NoData`, so a healthy network reads as no-data (C166's second defect, here alone);<br>- `gRPC telemetry stream lost`, `count by (source) (...) < 1`: **MISCODED, and the evidence is in its own data.** A lost source's series vanishes rather than reading 0, so the count can never be below 1; the source count fell from 4 to 2 in the last 7 days, and the rule recorded 5 `Normal (MissingSeries)` and never Alerting. It resolves exactly when it should fire;<br>- `Critical syslog received`: miscoded by naming (C166);<br>- `Interface error rate elevated`, rate > 0: inputs present (73 series), 0 for 7 days: quiet, and can fire;<br>- `Interface output discards`: fires (10 Alerting in 30 days);<br>- `IP SLA probe failing`, `!= 1` for 2 m: **WORKS** (measured at 1-minute resolution, 2026-09-28): its failures are one-minute timeouts (sense 4), 73 runs in 7 days across six probes, and the one run of 4 minutes (s3, 09-22 23:43) did go Pending, then Alerting, then Normal. "49 Pending, never Alerting" was read from a history capped at 100 rows (the window standing in for all time, mine); for the same reason "Device unreachable never alerted" is unproven.<br>**Every history verdict re-read WITHOUT A CAP (2026-09-28, the operator's sweep):** one day per request, and a window returning a full page split and re-read (the time unit is microseconds). Entered Alerting in 30 days: `Device unreachable (SNMP)` **38** ("never alerted" was FALSE, not merely unproven), `Interface output discards` **229** in 2,305 transitions (the capped read said 10: about 8 alerts a day is a rule a person learns to ignore, the drift checker's fate), `Interface down` 6, `Critical syslog received` 9, `IP SLA probe failing` 3, `gRPC telemetry stream lost` 0, `Interface error rate elevated` 0. The query-window verdicts (can fire, quiet this week, miscoded) stand as stated. **Two real incidents the old rules missed**, measured in the gRPC rule's own data: 09-22 23:41, about 20 minutes with NO telemetry source reporting (the same minutes as IP SLA's one long failure, so one fleet-level event seen by two rules), and 09-28 08:51 to 09:01, r1 and r3 absent. `count by (source) (...) < 1` recorded neither; the replacement fires on both (replayed before it was written).<br>**And the syslog stream's `host` label is the COLLECTOR** (`nmas` on all 3,151 lines in 24 h), so a syslog rule's instance carries no device: the heartbeat rules get it by matching the line (`\s<host>:\s`), and C166's rule cannot say which device. For 8.6 that is an instance with no member. **And the SNMP TARGETS are a hand-kept list too** (measured 2026-09-28 by the Grafana reader's address resolution, read-only on the host): the rules' polled `instance` addresses are nine, eight resolve to inventory devices, and `10.255.1.15` (r5, RETIRED) is still polled by two rules, so its alerts would read "matches no device's address in the inventory" (correctly); and **r6 is polled by none**, monitored by its heartbeat alone. Coverage inherited, not designed: a list current when written, r5 leaving and r6 arriving both after. | A4 | **NetBox records every device's platform as `ios`, the C8000v routers included, and NMAS wrote it.** Measured 2026-09-25: r1–r6 (C8000v, IOS-XE) and s1–s4 (vIOS-L2) are all `ios`. The NSoT manifest has them right. **The writer is `_parse_facts_from_config()`** (`netbox_client.py`, used by the golden-driven import). It **defaults to `"IOS"`** and switches to `IOS-XE` only if the config TEXT contains "IOS XE" or "IOS-XE". **A captured golden contains neither**: the live `golden/r2.cfg` has 0 occurrences. So every router falls back to the default, and `_ensure_platform()` slugs it `ios`. **The fleet fixtures each contain the string once**, so any test of this path passes against a fixture while the live store fails it: a fixture that cannot exhibit the case, again. `platform` is in the sync's update allowlist, so **every sync re-asserts `ios`**. A human correction in NetBox would be reverted on the next sync, and only the modification record would show it. A text-sniffing default written as a fact: the fix is to take the platform from the manifest's dialect through `platform.py`, the one translation, never from config text. Consequences: a NetBox-sourced list cannot resolve any device's platform (`ios` is not a slug `platform.py` maps), and anything keyed on NetBox's platform gives routers switch behaviour. Found because the heartbeat window has to be per platform. | **[C, F → 7.6; sorted 2026-09-28]** build | **7.6** (moved from the open sections 2026-09-28: a stage owns it). NSOT_PLAN.md P.1 (step 4, r2) |

| C14 | **A scheduled job can fail for a day and nothing says so.** Measured 2026-09-25: `clab-sync.service` failed **72 consecutive runs** (every 30 min from 2026-09-24 08:40; last success 08:10) with `nmas-clab-targets: command not found`. The helper was a `~/bin` symlink on the login PATH and not systemd's. The refusal was correct and went into a journal nobody read, and r6's startup config fell a day behind its branch-site config. Had the clab host rebooted, r6 would have booted management-only. **Fixed at the cause:** the sanitiser resolves its helpers beside its own real path, refuses by name when one is missing, and is deployed as a symlink to `scripts/oxidized-to-config.sh`. The deployed copy had also diverged from the repository's (C6 (4)). **The class is BUILT and not yet SURFACED:** `modules/job_health.py` (GET `/jobs/health`, `scripts/nmas-jobs`) reads systemd and the journal per declared job and reports ok / failing (the cause line, the streak) / stale / never_succeeded / not_installed (never ok: systemd reports `Result=success` for a unit that does not exist, measured) / unknown. Live: "0 of 3 job(s) ok". | C92 | **The online dot is one probe, five seconds old at best, and a single miss draws "offline"** (the operator, 2026-09-27). `connection.ping_worker` probes every device of the ACTIVE list every 5 s (`is_device_online`: ICMP with a 2 s timeout, then TCP:22 with a 2 s timeout) and overwrites an in-memory `device_status_cache[ip] = bool`: no count, no time, no history. **Measured from the host's app log**, which records every transition at INFO (7,061 transitions, 2026-09-23 07:52 to 09-27 17:59, 106 hours): 2,946 offline runs, and **2,751 of them (93%) ended at the very next probe**. s3 alone accounts for 1,540 runs (14.5 an hour, one every four minutes), and 1,362 of its 1,478 single-probe misses had no other device down at the same time. Every other device's traffic to the NMAS crosses s3's `Vlan99`, so s3 was forwarding while its own control plane did not answer in 2 s. The two switches with the slowest measured clocks (C9) miss most: s3 (0.55 to 0.61) 1,540 and s4 (0.75) 227, against 161 to 192 for every other device (r5, retired partway, 91). That is a correlation (a starved vIOS answers slowly), not a proven cause. **A threshold measured from those runs:** at two consecutive misses the fleet's offline runs fall from 2,946 to 195, and at three to 99; the log's whole-second times make that approximate, and a reader counting misses makes it exact. Genuine outages look different: runs over 60 s mostly come 6 to 8 devices at once (2026-09-26 00:12, 08:33 and 08:41; 2026-09-27 08:42), which is the path to the fleet rather than a device. One run (s3, 92 minutes, 09-27 02:34) is not attributed; the transition log cannot show whether the app itself stopped inside it. s3's own pattern is C93. **Four defects in one value:**<br>- one miss is "not answering right now", drawn as "offline";<br>- "online" is three claims, and the dot makes one without saying which: answered a probe from the NMAS within 2 s (this), the device's own heartbeat arrived in its window (P.1), and the tool can log in (SSH, phase 2's definition of reached);<br>- the dot has no age, so a stale value and a fresh one look the same, and `.get(ip, False)` draws a device never probed (another list, a NetBox-sourced list with no CSV, the first cycle) as offline (§14's finding, still true of the dot);<br>- it is not only a dot: `refresh_hostnames`, Auto-Create (a skip reason "offline"), the topology link-status reads (`/topology/link_status`, `/topology/proto_link_status`), the AI device context and the agent's bulk targeting all act on one probe.<br>**Cost at scale** (§0a): 900 devices every 5 s is 180 probes a second from one host, and a failed probe costs up to 4 s of a 20-thread pool, so a partial outage stretches the cycle it is measuring. **The case that sets the priority** (C70, the operator, 2026-09-27): r2's dot went offline immediately after the scoped restore changed r2's GigabitEthernet2, its core link, on the path in-band management uses (the change itself was IPv6-only). For about thirty seconds the operator believed the restore had cut in-band management. r2 was fine: ping 5 of 5, 23 to 31 ms. So the problem is not that the dot is noisy. It is noisy in a way indistinguishable from the failure people most fear, at the moment they are most primed to believe it: right after a change to the path the tool reaches the device on ("the repair path travels over the thing being changed"). **And on the deployment host the dot does not ping at all** (measured the same day): `ping3` raises `PermissionError` as the service user (`flask-app` runs as `<user>`, no capabilities, `net.ipv4.ping_group_range` is `1 0`). `is_device_online()` catches it, logs it at DEBUG, and falls through to TCP:22 with a 2 s timeout. So the dot's claim is "the device's SSH listener accepted a connection within 2 s", made under the name "ping", with the failure of the ICMP half invisible. The reader's claim must say which probe it made. | **[C, F → 7.3 (the drawing); sorted 2026-09-28; the reader BUILT 2026-09-28 as 7.2 step 19]** **The reader half is done** (`modules/readers/reachability.py`): every list's devices probed every 5 s, each with its last result, which probe answered, the consecutive-miss count against ONE threshold function (3, from the measurement above; P.8 makes it per network), and since when; it OWNS the dict every consumer already read (`device_status_cache` is its `STATUS`), so the dot, Refresh Hostnames, Auto-Create, the topology reads, the agent's context and the NetBox sync act on the thresholded answer without being edited; a device on another list is probed now; its liveness is a job-health row; Needs attention draws ONE row naming every device not answering. `ping_worker` is gone (`session_reaper` keeps its idle-session reaping). What remains is the drawing below, 7.3's, and it must name the claim (the operator, 2026-09-28): WHICH probe answered is a property of the environment, not the device, and on the host it is TCP 22 because the service user may not send ICMP, so a host where ICMP works would change what the dot means with nothing on screen saying so. The original plan: build: **one reader job, two consumers** (the operator's shape, the same as 8.6's alert reader). The reader lives with **7.2**'s job-health and one-reader work: it keeps, per device, the last result, when it was taken, and the consecutive-miss count, with the threshold taken from the measurement above (per device if the switches keep differing, as the heartbeat windows had to be), and it says which claim it makes. The **drawing** is **7.3**'s: the Device page and the device list show the claim with its age ("answering, checked 40 s ago"; "not answering for 3 probes, since 18:04"; "not checked": never "offline" for a device nobody asked). The actions above read the reader's state rather than one probe. **The reader records its own liveness** (the time of its last completed cycle, and a job-health row of its own, as the Grafana reader needs), because a reader that logs only transitions cannot tell its own absence from its subject's: s3's 92-minute gap on 09-27 cannot be attributed for exactly that reason (C93). | **7.2** (moved from the open sections 2026-09-28: a stage owns it). this file, 2026-09-27 |

| C106 | **CLIs that stop half-way, leaving a state nobody is told about** (the operator's question after `nmas-deploy` moved the checkout and could not restart, 2026-09-27; surveyed by reading every script, 1a VERIFIED and FIXED, the rest from the code read and not yet verified). (1a) `rotate()` set "rotated and committed" whatever `_commit` answered and then DELETED the staging copy, so a failed credential-store write left a device holding a password recorded nowhere, reported as "live and recorded": fixed (state `ROTATED_NOT_RECORDED`, the copy kept and named, danger first, a job-health row). Recovery is by hand from the kept copy: a `--record-staged` path does not exist yet. (1b) the persist chain's `oxidized_row` ran `sudo -n` with no pre-check (the operator, next after C70: an unchecked sudo in the rotation path is B15's shape). Verified: a refused `sudo -n` printed nothing on stdout, the stage parsed `{}`, and the chain failed with NO reason after the device was already rotated and committed. **FIXED 2026-09-27**: preflight asks `sudo -n -l <helper>` before anything changes the device and refuses naming the exact sudoers line; the stage checks sudo's exit code and names a refusal; an answer without `ok` is not success. Measured on the host: the narrow rule exists and the service runs as the user it covers, so nothing is refused there today. (The danger sentence printing last remains.) (2) `oxidized-to-config.sh` can overwrite some labs' startup configs and then fail a later copy, the read-back or the commit; a failed backup is ignored (`cp -r … 2>/dev/null`), and a failed commit still ends "Startup-configs updated". (3) `nmas-retire` clears the credential before a step that can fail, and its refusal omits what was already done (it is resumable). (4) `nmas-netbox-backup` pruned old backups before the new one had shipped, and by AGE alone: while shipping failed, the local copies were the only copies and were deleted as they aged, so a failed run left fewer backups than before (the operator: fix first). **FIXED 2026-09-27**: ship first, prune last, and an expired backup is deleted only once recorded as shipped to every destination its tier needs (`shipped.json`); an unshipped one is kept, named in the run and first in `--status`, and retried each run. **Deletion is keyed on the PROPERTY, not a proxy for it** (the operator): age was the proxy, existence elsewhere is the property, the register's recurring lesson applied to retention. And the retry converts a permanent gap into a delay: a missed daily used to be lost for good and now ships late, so the off-box copy's completeness no longer depends on nothing having failed on any given night. (5) `nmas-bulk-intent` and three `nsot_*` repair scripts leave written, uncommitted files, reported as "REFUSED", which reads as nothing having happened (the standing rule inverted: the message says nothing happened, the filesystem disagrees, and the person acts on the message). **Bulk intent FIXED 2026-09-27**: a failed commit puts every written file back as committed and says "nothing changed", or leads with LEFT UNCOMMITTED naming the paths; the three repair scripts remain. Also: `nmas-deploy` exits with a traceback and no audit row if `git fetch` or `merge` fails; `nmas-persist-native` exits 1 ("not persisted") if recording raises after the device saved. **The pattern (the operator's):** a tool that does part of an operation and stops, and tells the person running it but not the exit code or anything that watches. `nmas-deploy` is fixed (it refuses before moving when it cannot restart, exit 6; a failed restart leads with MIXED VERSION; a `running-version` job-health row). **The ordering, checked against each remaining item** (the operator's question after 1b, 2026-09-27: does the irreversible part come first, with the blocker found second?). Read from the code: **(2) the containerlab sync: YES, three times.** A lab's startup configs are overwritten, and a LATER lab's copy failure is found afterwards. The loop continues and the count check refuses with exit 1, while the first lab stays overwritten and uncommitted. The next run's reconcile can record it late. The per-lab backup runs as `cp -r … 2>/dev/null; rsync …`. The `;` means a failed backup does not stop the overwrite it exists to protect. Whether a destination can commit (repo present, git identity) is found only after every file is written. The run then ends with "Startup-configs updated." as its LAST line, after the failure block. The fix has three parts. Check every destination's preconditions before copying any. Gate each rsync on its backup (`&&`). Lead with the state. **(3) nmas-retire: YES.** It clears the device's credential override FIRST (irreversible: the value is gone), then writes the declaration, then commits. The commit can fail (`manifest.release`, git), and on failure the device is still in the inventory, managed, with its override gone. The CLI then leads with "REFUSED:", which reads as nothing having happened. The fix is to commit first (the commit already puts the tree back on failure), delete both credentials last, and have a stop lead with what was done. **(5) the three repair scripts: NO, the same symptom by a different mechanism.** Nothing irreversible happens before the commit. They write working-tree files, and a failed commit leaves them written. The fix is bulk intent's: put the files back as committed. **`nmas-persist-native`: NO.** The device save is the operation, and a failed record after it is a false REPORT ("not persisted"), not a late-found blocker. **`nmas-deploy`'s fetch/merge: NO.** A failed `merge --ff-only` leaves the checkout unmoved, so only the traceback is wrong. **1b's danger sentence printing last** is the report half of the same shape, and stays open. **(2) the containerlab sync, FIXED 2026-09-27 (the operator's order: the `;` first):** a lab is copied only after its OWN backup succeeded (the `;` let a failed backup go on to overwrite the configs it protected). A failure is named for its step: file list, BACKUP FAILED, or COPY FAILED after the backup (restore from it). Attempts are counted apart from successes, so a failed lab is no longer blamed on the loop ("ran fewer times") or the copy ("reported success ... do not match"), both false. Only the copied labs are read back and committed. The staged files are kept while any lab failed, and the summary leads with "NOT ALL LABS UPDATED: N of M" and exits 3. Tested by executing the shipped region under bash against real directories, with one lab's backup refused; a control that stops the failed backup from stopping its copy fails both tests. **The second half, FIXED the same day (the operator: fix it with the rest; the third script with this shape):** before any copy, each destination is asked whether it can take a commit (its git directory writable, no `index.lock` left behind; the identity rides on every commit already). A lab that cannot is refused BEFORE its copy, named "CANNOT COMMIT (reason); NOTHING was copied". A lab with no repository is still copied and reported NOT VERSIONED, a declared state named every run. Tested with a repository holding a stale `index.lock`; control: the skip removed fails it. | **[C, F → 7.3 for (3): retire clears the credential before a commit that can fail, so a failed retire leaves a device MANAGED WITH NO CREDENTIAL (A if it fires); 7.3 builds retire as an operation, commit first, and (3) is its acceptance. The rest, M: (1b)'s danger sentence printing last, (5)'s three repair scripts, `nmas-persist-native`'s false report, `nmas-deploy`'s traceback. (2) is FIXED (bfde4ef, d7efb9d). Re-sorted 2026-09-28 (it was UNKNOWN, sorted from a truncated read; see the Re-sort section)]** build: each, in the order above; verify each before fixing (a code read is a claim); the rule for all: check what the later step needs before the earlier step, and a stop's FIRST line names the state and the one command | **7.3** (moved from the open sections 2026-09-28: a stage owns it). this file, 2026-09-27 |

The asks: the cause confirmed, not assumed; the CLASS (every commit site in a list's repository, and which push); pushing made a property of committing, with a scan that fails the suite on a commit outside the chokepoint; "N commit(s) not pushed" with the oldest one's age on the Remote card and in Needs attention, derived from HEAD against the remote ref, never from the hook's own record (which also catches C172's symptom). `7a72258` stays unpushed until the fix is deployed, so the fix is seen closing a real gap. Nothing sensitive: onboarding intent carries secret names only. | 2026-09-29 | **FIXED, built the same evening; the operator's host run to come (7a72258 left unpushed until the deploy, so the fix is seen closing a real gap).** **The cause, confirmed from the code:** `abandon_onboarding` committed with `_repo.git(repo, "-c", ..., "commit", ...)` and never called `hooks.run_post_commit`; onboarding's commit went through `save_host_vars` -> `_commit_paths`, which does. **The sweep, every commit in a list's repository:** through the hook already: `save_golden` (and the empty decision commit), which carries capture and Save All, the batch deploy, restore with its intent, and rotation's golden; and `_commit_paths` (`save_templates`, `save_host_vars`: templates, intent edits, seed, revert, bulk intent; retire directly); BYPASSING it: abandon, the rename commit (`apply_pending_renames`, the Sync device names path), both migration commits, and `config_git`'s first commit; `init_repo`'s first commit and .gitignore top-up are carried by the write path's own commit, declared. **Structural now:** `repo.commit()` is THE commit, and it publishes; `repo.publish()` is called directly only by a caller that tags between committing and pushing; `tests/test_every_commit_publishes.py` fails on any git call naming `commit` outside it (an AST scan of the program and its scripts, with a floor, subprocess lists included), on a deferred commit its caller never publishes, and on `run_post_commit` reached around `publish()`; controls: abandon put back fails the scan and the real-abandon test, `_commit_paths` without `publish()` fails the deferral rule. **Visible:** the `remote-publication` reader compares HEAD with the remote's own branch (`git ls-remote` through the repository's own `origin`, 10 s bound, measured 0.99 s on the host), every 120 s and after every commit (a post-commit hook after the push); its one sentence (`remote_publication.describe()`) is drawn by the Git tab ("Everything is committed · 1 commit(s) not pushed to <remote> (oldest: <sha>, 12 min)", or "and pushed"), the Remote card, and a Needs attention row with the push as its action. The matching hashes proved only that the same bytes were copied. **Measured afterwards, read-only:** (1) the laptop holds the REAL key: `gpg -K` shows `sec`/`ssb` with no `#` or `>`, both keygrips are agent type D (disk) with protection P, and no smartcard is configured. (2) The 849-byte export is NOT a stub (the operator's reading, corrected): parsed in an empty keyring, both packets carry iter+salt S2K-protected secret material, never `gnu-dummy` or divert-to-card, and 849 bytes is normal for ed25519 plus cv25519. So the failure is in the IMPORT, not the export: a mistyped passphrase or GnuPG's own conversion of this subkey, not yet told apart. (3) **The restore test decrypts nothing**: `nmas-netbox-restore-test` restores the newest LOCAL hourly backup, which is plain. It proves the dump restores and says nothing about the encrypted copies. With B9's Object Lock the off-box copies can no longer be deleted, which makes them safe AND unreadable. Measured on the laptop: key `38AB129536505C0A7BE4995D5434D4489191526F`, both subkeys passphrase-protected (gpg-agent `P`), and `~/nmas-backup-paperkey.txt` names the same key. The laptop's `b2:` remote downloads a real backup at its listed size (366,283 bytes). **The fix is a copy, not a build:** the exported secret key (still passphrase-protected) goes to the Proxmox host (<hypervisor>, root by key, gpg present), a failure domain the laptop does not share. A root there already holds the VM disks and their plain local backups, so the copy costs no confidentiality. It is PROVEN only by importing the copy, fetched back from Proxmox, into an EMPTY keyring and decrypting a real B2 backup with it: a copy of the wrong key looks exactly like a working one, and the decrypt also proves the passphrase is known. The passphrase must itself survive the laptop. docs/NETBOX_BACKUP.md §1 |

| C120 | **A negative control's restore left stale bytecode, and the next run executed the MUTATED code** (found fixing C114, 2026-09-27). The control changed `_NEIGHBOR_DROP_TOLERANCE = 0` to `1` (same size) and a `cp` restored it within the same second. Python validates a cached `.pyc` by the source's mtime (1 s resolution) and size, so the restored file matched the mutated bytecode, and the run after the restore had tolerance 1 while the file said 0. The following control then failed a test it did not target, which is what exposed it. C42's shape, a control whose timing is tighter than the clock, in the controls themselves. Controls earlier in this session were checked: every other mutation changed the file's size.  **Which past controls could it have touched? Reproduced in a scratch directory, 2026-09-27, and bounded rather than counted** (the operator's question 1: about 330 one-time controls across weeks, and a size-preserving edit such as 0->1, true->false or a swapped operator is the natural shape of a control): **The control's own verdict is sound.** The mutated run executes the mutation, because the mutation moved the file's mtime into a new second, so its cached bytecode no longer matches. **The stale bytecode lands in the NEXT run after the restore,** and only when the restore falls in the same second as the mutation. That means mutate, run and restore all inside one second: a short targeted run, never a full suite (about 2 min). **Its consequence is a wrong post-restore run.** It is visible when that run exercises the mutation (a test fails, which is how C120 was found). It is silent when it does not: a laptop "N passed" after a control can have been partly about mutated code. **No committed code was tested that way.** Every commit's CI runs a fresh checkout with no bytecode, and every commit carrying code passed CI (232d001 aside, C119). So the claims resting on CI hold. **The other failure direction is loud, not silent.** A mutation made in the same second as a previous write of the ORIGINAL file could run the original, but that shows up as a control that did not fire, which was always investigated. **Which past instances were affected is unknowable:** the timing was never recorded. What is knowable is the bound above: control verdicts valid, CI verdicts valid, and some laptop post-restore counts possibly not. From now on, caching is off in the runner, so the question cannot arise again. | 2026-09-28 | **Moved 2026-09-28 by the triage, on the row's own evidence:** **FIXED:** `scripts/nmas-test` exports `PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1`, so no test run writes bytecode and a mutation can never be cached (full suite 114 s against 110-115 s before: no measurable cost; 0 `.pyc` written). Pinned in the running process by `test_network_guard.py` Where: this file, 2026-09-27 |

| C142 | `nmas-deploy` refused an empty commit on a green one, saying it "changes more than ignored paths ... : []": the empty case fell out of a rule written for the non-empty one, and the message printed the evidence against its own verdict (the operator, 2026-09-28, blocked deploying 41a4149, the corrected message for 17e9c7d). | 2026-09-28 | **Fixed.** `only_ignored()` is `bool(paths) and all(...)`, a correct predicate about a non-empty set, and nothing decided what the GATE does when nothing changed, so it inherited the predicate's answer. The inverse the operator asked about: an empty commit on a commit CI never saw still refuses (tested), because an unchanged tree proves nothing unless the tree it matches PASSED; and a tree that differs with no changed file named refuses as unknown. A revert back to a green tree deploys, by the same identity. Control: identity removed, the two aimed tests fail. `test_nmas_deploy.py::TestNothingChanged`. |

| E7 | A baseline recorded configuration, not a working network, so it could not be a "golden state". | 2026-09-27 | **Built, as the operator decided.** `modules/nsot/golden_state.py` judges every routing protocol a device's COMMITTED intent declares, from real output: OSPF and OSPFv3 (every neighbour FULL or 2WAY), BGP (every configured peer established), RIP (a gateway in RIP's own table), and RIPng (a next hop heard). RIPng's evidence was chosen by measurement: r1 hears s1 and installs nothing, since OSPFv3 wins on distance, so "learned a route" would have called it broken. It fails closed with reasons: a device not read, no committed intent, a declared protocol the device does not run, or one the tool does not measure. The baseline tag carries the claim and the snapshot; `golden-state/<ts>` marks a commit only when every device is working. `list_baselines()` reports each baseline's claim, the eleven older ones read as "configured", and the Baselines panel draws the claim. Verify's reader gained OSPFv3 and RIPng, so deploys check them too. `scripts/nmas-golden-state` takes one (dry-run by default; `--commit` writes it, `Actor:` the OS user, `Tool:` itself). **It names what the claim rests on** (the operator, 2026-09-27): every routing peer that is not an address of a managed device is listed as outside management, since its state is observed from one side only. RIPng's link-local next hops are named as unattributed, not guessed. Every tag carries a limits line: the tool sees what the managed devices report about themselves. **Dry-run on the live fleet 2026-09-27: "configured and working, resting on 4 routing peer(s) outside management"**: r3's and r4's IPv4 and IPv6 sessions to r5, retired from management. The baselines payload fixture is still empty (C72), so the claim badge is tested by executing its renderer directly. Controls: any OSPF state as up, BGP peers not needing to be established, the fleet always working, a golden-state tag regardless, and an old baseline claiming working each fail their aimed tests. |
| C74 | The golden state (and verify) judged BGP from `show ip bgp summary`, IPv4 sessions only, while r3's and r4's intent declares an IPv6 peer each; and reading both families would have read phantom peers. | 2026-09-27 | **Fixed, found by reading r3's intent beside the judgement.** Verify and the golden state read `show bgp all summary`, and each peer records its address family (r3 and r4: two sessions each, measured, including r4's REAL wrapped IPv6 row). The looser row rule would have read three phantom down peers from the lines between two families' tables ("0 BGP route-map cache entries using 0 bytes of memory" has ten fields and a digit first; measured). A row is now an address followed by BGP version 4, and each family resets the table. | C75 | A restore could CHANGE a credential the device holds: the deploy's rule ("a deploy may add an account; it may never change one") was never applied to it. | 2026-09-27 | **Fixed, found building the restore preview's gates part** (a gate has to state something true, and "credential unchanged" was not checked). Measured before the fix: a stored config holding the pre-rotation `secret 5` was `deployable: True`, and its program was the old `username` line alone, a lockout of the tool from a device it manages. `RestoreTarget` had no `capture_credentials`, so the guard would have seen nothing even if called, and nothing called it. The Baselines panel warned and asked for an acknowledgement, and the acknowledgement let it through. Now `RestoreTarget.checks` names the restore's own gates (stored config, printable ASCII, credential unchanged), `blocking_reasons` derives from them, and the preview draws the same list: one computation, read twice. The refusal names the restore and the form, never the value (a same-form change says "with a different value", because two identical forms read as no difference). `baseline_credential_gaps()` asks a REAL `RestoreTarget` whether it refuses, so `silent` is now what the restore would actually let through: an account the ref has and the device lacks, ADDED back, which is not a lockout, and the panel now says so instead of "NMAS would LOSE ACCESS". Control: the credential check reporting pass fails four aimed tests. **It was LIVE, and C70 was about to be the run that fired it** (the operator, 2026-09-27). The newest baseline predates s1's rotation. From the UI, the only way to restore from a baseline is the Baselines panel's whole-fleet button. So "run the restore once, deliberately" would, through that button, have pushed s1's old credential back onto the device, past a warning that could be acknowledged. That credential is the one B13 exposed. It lives in browser scrollback, s1's command history and a conversation, and it had further copies in seven baselines that nothing was guarding. The path that had never run was more dangerous than anyone knew, which is the strongest argument yet for running it once, deliberately. **Survey of the host's baselines, 2026-09-27 (read-only, by hash, no value printed):** all **11** carry an account-credential set for s1 that differs from what s1 holds now. **7** of them (`20260921T183320Z` to `20260925T201032Z`) carry the set s1 held from its 2026-09-21 18:33 rotation until `e1e8e49` (2026-09-26), which by date is the one B13 exposed. The 4 oldest carry an earlier set for s1, and for up to seven other devices as well. Every device except r6 has exactly one stale set in the baselines, and s1 has two. Of the 47 per-device `golden/*` tags, 21 differ from HEAD, 5 of them r5's (retired). No baseline's other secret positions differ from HEAD: no community, key-string or line password has ever been rotated (C79). **A baseline is a record of a moment, and moments contain credentials: any re-apply is a time machine for secrets as well as for configuration.** Nothing here is deleted. Golden history is verbatim by design, and only rotation makes a copy harmless. What changed is that a re-apply can no longer bring one back onto a device. |

All three are now keyed on (section, line). The check that settles it: plan each device and look for intent that differs from the capture beside an empty program (the plan's `intent vs capture` operand against `Nothing will be sent`). |
| C77 | `/deploy/plan` and `/golden/restore/preview` returned stored and rendered config verbatim: a planted community came back in both, as residue and as a line the program adds. | 2026-09-27 | **Fixed for both routes, and the sweep SCHEDULED** (the operator: two were found by drawing screens, and the next will be found by somebody's browser unless the sweep happens). `outbound.mask_payload()` masks the response after every hash is computed from the truthful program. The sweep's population comes from the gate table rather than a hand list, so a new computing POST is swept by being declared. The sweep adds what a POST reads that a GET does not: a device's running config as a read returns it now, a second backup, configs a caller supplies, a FakeNetBox, and a list that has what a real list has (seeded templates, r1's intent extracted and committed through the routes, the template approved, a template secret that differs from the device, a second golden). A refusal proves nothing, so the eight routes whose response draws stored config must show the masked slot (either mask). Each route is driven anonymously and as a person. **Its first real run found three leaks, from two stores, fixed here:** `POST /compare_backups` returned both backups' secrets in its diff, and `POST /netbox/safety/import/preview` and `/import_all/preview` returned each device's golden and SNMP community. The previews' community was a plain VALUE (`snmp.communities[].community`), which positional redaction cannot see, so `mask_payload` now also masks by key (`SECRET_FIELDS`, exact names). The previews led to C95: NetBox itself holds the secrets. Controls, each restored from a copy: the compare's mask removed, the key masking removed, and the deploy plan's mask removed (the sweep then finds both halves of C77 on its own: the golden as residue, and the template secret in an added line). Also found: the sweep's drift check left a result in the process-wide checker, which a later test read. The sweep now gets a checker of its own, and the leak exposed C96. |
